I really can’t honestly call it occasional anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you mean by a 'secret eating spell'?\n\nPatient: Well, it usually happens when I'm alone. Like, after a bridge night with my friends or when I get off a late shift at the store. I'll come home and... I just start eating. It's... it's much more food than I would ever put on a plate if someone else was in the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It really bothers me. If I'm getting ready for church and my skirt feels even a little tight, or if I catch a glimpse of my side view in one of the mirrors at the store... it honestly just ruins my whole day. I start feeling like everyone is looking at me and thinking I've completely let myself go. It's just a constant worry for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even when you're at a weight that others might consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I don't really know what's normal for me anymore. People tell me I look fine, but... I just don't see it that way. Even if the scale says a number that's supposed to be okay for my height, I still feel heavy. I just can't seem to feel comfortable with it, no matter what anyone else thinks.\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight when you look in the mirror, even if others say you are at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I don't know if I look in the mirror and see someone who is completely overweight overall. I just see all the areas that bother me. \n\nLike, I'll focus right in on my stomach or my arms, and to me, they just look too big. I know what size clothes I buy for work, so I know I'm not huge, but... I just don't see the healthy person my friends say they see. I just see the parts of myself that I want to fix. But it’s gotten to the point where it isn’t just a one-time mistake anymore, and I knew I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, for at least the past several months, I'd say. It’s been happening most weeks now. I'll have one of those secret eating spells, and then I just go make myself sick right afterward. I really can’t honestly call it occasional anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you mean by a 'secret eating spell'?\n\nPatient: Well, it usually happens when I'm alone. Like, after a bridge night with my friends or when I get off a late shift at the store. I'll come home and... I just start eating. I might have leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe a bowl of ice cream. And I eat it all in one sitting. It's... it's much more food than I would ever put on a plate if someone else was in the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It really bothers me. If I'm getting ready for church and my skirt feels even a little tight, or if I catch a glimpse of my side view in one of the mirrors at the store... it honestly just ruins my whole day. I start feeling like everyone is looking at me and thinking I've completely let myself go. It's just a constant worry for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even when you're at a weight that others might consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I don't really know what's normal for me anymore. People tell me I look fine, but... I just don't see it that way. Even if the scale says a number that's supposed to be okay for my height, I still feel heavy. I just can't seem to feel comfortable with it, no matter what anyone else thinks.\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight when you look in the mirror, even if others say you are at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I don't know if I look in the mirror and see someone who is completely overweight overall. I just see all the areas that bother me. \n\nLike, I'll focus right in on my stomach or my arms, and to me, they just look too big. I know what size clothes I buy for work, so I know I'm not huge, but... I just don't see the healthy person my friends say they see. I just see the parts of myself that I want to fix. So I guess I don't really know how to answer that, other than I just never like what I see.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you think your body shape or weight is the way you actually see it in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know. I mean, I see what I see, but... I don't know if it's the reality of how I actually look. I just know what I focus on, and that's what seems real to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if I eat a lot like that, I always end up throwing it up. I don't just eat a bunch of food and leave it at that. I always feel like I have to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: I think you might have just asked me that, but no. Like I was saying, if I eat a huge amount of food all at once, I always make myself sick afterward. I never just eat like that and keep it down. I always have to get rid of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

{"intent": "determine if the patient has a fear of gaining weight due to a distorted body image", "question": "Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't fear gaining weight?"}

I mean... I don't know if I look in the mirror and see someone who is completely overweight overall. I just see all the areas that bother me. \n\nLike, I'll focus right in on my stomach or my arms, and to me, they just look too big. I know what size clothes I buy for work, so I know I'm not huge, but... I just don't see the healthy person my friends say they see. I just see the parts of myself that I want to fix. So I guess I don't really know how to answer that, other than I just never like what I see.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you think your body shape or weight is the way you actually see it in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know. I mean, I see what I see, but... I don't know if it's the reality of how I actually look. I just know what I focus on, and that's what seems real to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if I eat a lot like that, I always end up throwing it up. I don't just eat a bunch of food and leave it at that. I always feel like I have to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: I think you might have just asked me that, but no. Like I was saying, if I eat a huge amount of food all at once, I always make myself sick afterward. I never just eat like that and keep it down. I always have to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... probably not? The whole reason I do it is because I'm so scared of putting on weight. I guess if I didn't have that fear, I wouldn't feel the need to get rid of the food. But it's hard to even imagine not worrying about it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you knew you wouldn't gain any weight from it?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, doctor. But, um... no, my answer is the same. If I knew for an absolute fact that I wouldn't gain any weight from the food, I wouldn't make myself sick. Once I start during those times, it just... it feels like I'm on automatic. I'll be sitting there thinking I really should stop before my husband notices, but I just keep reaching for one more thing. But it’s gotten to the point where it isn’t just a one-time mistake anymore, and I knew I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, for at least the past several months, I'd say. It’s been happening most weeks now. I'll have one of those secret eating spells, and then I just go make myself sick right afterward. I really can’t honestly call it occasional anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you mean by a 'secret eating spell'?\n\nPatient: Well, it usually happens when I'm alone. Like, after a bridge night with my friends or when I get off a late shift at the store. I'll come home and... I just start eating. I might have leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe a bowl of ice cream. And I eat it all in one sitting. It's... it's much more food than I would ever put on a plate if someone else was in the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It really bothers me. If I'm getting ready for church and my skirt feels even a little tight, or if I catch a glimpse of my side view in one of the mirrors at the store... it honestly just ruins my whole day. I start feeling like everyone is looking at me and thinking I've completely let myself go. It's just a constant worry for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even when you're at a weight that others might consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I don't really know what's normal for me anymore. People tell me I look fine, but... I just don't see it that way. Even if the scale says a number that's supposed to be okay for my height, I still feel heavy. I just can't seem to feel comfortable with it, no matter what anyone else thinks.\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight when you look in the mirror, even if others say you are at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I don't know if I look in the mirror and see someone who is completely overweight overall. I just see all the areas that bother me. \n\nLike, I'll focus right in on my stomach or my arms, and to me, they just look too big. So I guess I don't really know how to answer that, other than I just never like what I see.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you think your body shape or weight is the way you actually see it in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello. I guess what finally scared me into making an appointment is... well, after I eat too much, I’ve been making myself throw up in the bathroom. I just do it so I won’t gain weight. But it’s gotten to the point where it isn’t just a one-time mistake anymore, and I knew I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, for at least the past several months, I'd say. It’s been happening most weeks now. I'll have one of those secret eating spells, and then I just go make myself sick right afterward. I really can’t honestly call it occasional anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you mean by a 'secret eating spell'?\n\nPatient: Well, it usually happens when I'm alone. Like, after a bridge night with my friends or when I get off a late shift at the store. I'll come home and... I just start eating. I might have leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe a bowl of ice cream. And I eat it all in one sitting. It's... it's much more food than I would ever put on a plate if someone else was in the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It really bothers me. If I'm getting ready for church and my skirt feels even a little tight, or if I catch a glimpse of my side view in one of the mirrors at the store... it honestly just ruins my whole day. I start feeling like everyone is looking at me and thinking I've completely let myself go. It's just a constant worry for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even when you're at a weight that others might consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I don't really know what's normal for me anymore. People tell me I look fine, but... I just don't see it that way. Even if the scale says a number that's supposed to be okay for my height, I still feel heavy. I just can't seem to feel comfortable with it, no matter what anyone else thinks.\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight when you look in the mirror, even if others say you are at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I don't know if I look in the mirror and see someone who is completely overweight overall. I just see all the areas that bother me. \n\nLike, I'll focus right in on my stomach or my arms, and to me, they just look too big. I know what size clothes I buy for work, so I know I'm not huge, but... I just don't see the healthy person my friends say they see. I just see the parts of myself that I want to fix. So I guess I don't really know how to answer that, other than I just never like what I see.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you think your body shape or weight is the way you actually see it in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know. I mean, I see what I see, but... I don't know if it's the reality of how I actually look. I just know what I focus on, and that's what seems real to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if I eat a lot like that, I always end up throwing it up. I don't just eat a bunch of food and leave it at that. I always feel like I have to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: I think you might have just asked me that, but no. Like I was saying, if I eat a huge amount of food all at once, I always make myself sick afterward. I never just eat like that and keep it down. I always have to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... probably not? The whole reason I do it is because I'm so scared of putting on weight. I guess if I didn't have that fear, I wouldn't feel the need to get rid of the food. But it's hard to even imagine not worrying about it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you knew you wouldn't gain any weight from it?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, doctor. But, um... no, my answer is the same. If I knew for an absolute fact that I wouldn't gain any weight from the food, I wouldn't make myself sick. Keeping the weight off is really the only reason I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating during these secret eating spells?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Once I start during those times, it just... it feels like I'm on automatic. I'll be sitting there thinking I really should stop before my husband notices, but I just keep reaching for one more thing. I can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I know we just went over this a minute ago, but yes, that's exactly it. That fear is the whole reason I do it. If I wasn't so worried about the scale going up, I wouldn't be making myself sick. It really just comes down to the weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't make yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I ever have. If I have one of those spells where I eat a whole lot of food really fast, I always make myself sick afterward. I just... I couldn't stand to let all that sit in my stomach. So, no, I always do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is distorted?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure if distorted is the right word, but it definitely takes over my thoughts. Like... if my skirt feels even a little tight before church, or if I happen to catch my side view in one of the mirrors at the store, it can just ruin my whole day. I immediately start feeling like everyone around me can tell I’ve let myself go. So, yes, how my shape looks is something I focus on a great deal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape is actually the way you see it?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. It's... it's much more food than I would ever put on a plate if someone else was in the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It really bothers me. If I'm getting ready for church and my skirt feels even a little tight, or if I catch a glimpse of my side view in one of the mirrors at the store... it honestly just ruins my whole day. I start feeling like everyone is looking at me and thinking I've completely let myself go. It's just a constant worry for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even when you're at a weight that others might consider healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I don't really know what's normal for me anymore. People tell me I look fine, but... I just don't see it that way. Even if the scale says a number that's supposed to be okay for my height, I still feel heavy. I just can't seem to feel comfortable with it, no matter what anyone else thinks.\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight when you look in the mirror, even if others say you are at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I don't know if I look in the mirror and see someone who is completely overweight overall. I just see all the areas that bother me. \n\nLike, I'll focus right in on my stomach or my arms, and to me, they just look too big. I know what size clothes I buy for work, so I know I'm not huge, but... I just don't see the healthy person my friends say they see. I just see the parts of myself that I want to fix. So I guess I don't really know how to answer that, other than I just never like what I see.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you think your body shape or weight is the way you actually see it in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know. I mean, I see what I see, but... I don't know if it's the reality of how I actually look. I just know what I focus on, and that's what seems real to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if I eat a lot like that, I always end up throwing it up. I don't just eat a bunch of food and leave it at that. I always feel like I have to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: I think you might have just asked me that, but no. Like I was saying, if I eat a huge amount of food all at once, I always make myself sick afterward. I never just eat like that and keep it down. I always have to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... probably not? The whole reason I do it is because I'm so scared of putting on weight. I guess if I didn't have that fear, I wouldn't feel the need to get rid of the food. But it's hard to even imagine not worrying about it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you knew you wouldn't gain any weight from it?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, doctor. But, um... no, my answer is the same. If I knew for an absolute fact that I wouldn't gain any weight from the food, I wouldn't make myself sick. Keeping the weight off is really the only reason I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating during these secret eating spells?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Once I start during those times, it just... it feels like I'm on automatic. I'll be sitting there thinking I really should stop before my husband notices, but I just keep reaching for one more thing. I can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I know we just went over this a minute ago, but yes, that's exactly it. That fear is the whole reason I do it. If I wasn't so worried about the scale going up, I wouldn't be making myself sick. It really just comes down to the weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't make yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I ever have. If I have one of those spells where I eat a whole lot of food really fast, I always make myself sick afterward. I just... I couldn't stand to let all that sit in my stomach. So, no, I always do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is distorted?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure if distorted is the right word, but it definitely takes over my thoughts. Like... if my skirt feels even a little tight before church, or if I happen to catch my side view in one of the mirrors at the store, it can just ruin my whole day. I immediately start feeling like everyone around me can tell I’ve let myself go. So, yes, how my shape looks is something I focus on a great deal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape is actually the way you see it?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... when I look in the mirror, I see what I see, and it feels completely real to me. But I suppose... I'm just not sure if that's how I actually look to everyone else. It's hard to tell what the reality is when you worry about it as much as I do. So, I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape is the same as how others see it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... like I was just saying, I really don't know. I mean, I work the floor at the store all day, so I'm around people constantly, but... I have no idea if they see the same thing I see in the mirror. I just can't really tell what they're thinking when they look at me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
